<td valign="top"><strong>Question:</strong> Which option best describes the catalytic activity associated with this protein?<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Hydrolyzes L-glutamine to L-glutamate and ammonia; the ammonia supports FGAR conversion to FGAM in the purine-biosynthesis complex.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Transfers an amino group from L-glutamine to phosphoribosyl pyrophosphate to form phosphoribosylamine in purine biosynthesis.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Converts FGAR directly to FGAM using ATP, carrying out both glutamine hydrolysis and the ATP-dependent amidination within one catalytic subunit.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Ligates L-glutamate and ammonia to form L-glutamine using ATP, replenishing glutamine for purine biosynthesis.<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: The protein sequence (~240 amino acids) contains the characteristic &quot;CNG&quot; motif (specifically &quot;CNGFQILQEL&quot;), which is the signature of a class I glutamine amidotransferase (GATase) domain. The small size (~240 aa) is too short to be a full FGAM synthetase (PurL, ~520 aa) or a bifunctional PurL-PurQ fusion protein, but matches the size of PurQ — the standalone glutamine amidotransferase subunit of the FGAM synthetase complex (PurL-PurQ) in purine biosynthesis.<br><br>Evaluating each option:<br>- Option A describes hydrolysis of glutamine to glutamate + ammonia, with the ammonia supporting FGAR→FGAM conversion — this is precisely the role of PurQ (GATase subunit).<br>- Option B describes PurF (amidophosphoribosyltransferase), a different enzyme acting on PRPP.<br>- Option C describes a bifunctional enzyme with both activities in one subunit — inconsistent with the small GATase-only domain size.<br>- Option D describes glutamine synthetase (GlnA), which is unrelated to this domain.<br><br>The GATase motif and protein size confirm this is PurQ, whose catalytic activity is glutamine hydrolysis to supply ammonia for the FGAM synthetase complex.<br><br>2. answer: A</td>

Which proposed activity best matches this protein? Compare the reactants and products in each alternative rather than assuming that reactions in the same pathway are catalyzed by the same component.<br><br><strong>Choices:</strong><br><br><strong>A.</strong> Hydrolyzes L-glutamine to L-glutamate and ammonia, supplying ammonia for FGAR conversion to FGAM by another component.<br><br><strong>B.</strong> Transfers an amino group from L-glutamine to phosphoribosyl pyrophosphate, forming phosphoribosylamine.<br><br><strong>C.</strong> Converts FGAR directly to FGAM using ATP, performing both glutamine hydrolysis and amidination within one catalytic subunit.<br><br><strong>D.</strong> Uses ATP to ligate L-glutamate and ammonia, forming L-glutamine.<br><br><strong>Skills:</strong> catalytic_activity_0008:1, catalytic_activity_0003:2<br><br><strong>Flash-Next response:</strong><br><br><br><br>1. reasoning: <br><br>**Step 1: Analyze the protein sequence.**<br>The sequence is approximately 270 amino acids long and contains motifs characteristic of a glutamine amidotransferase (GATase) domain:<br>- The region &quot;GEGNFYCDDE&quot; contains a potential catalytic cysteine residue, which is the hallmark of class I GATase domains.<br>- The overall fold and length are consistent with a standalone GATase domain.<br><br>**Step 2: Consider the biological context.**<br>The question specifies a &quot;multicomponent biosynthetic system&quot; and references FGAR (formylglycinamide ribonucleotide) to FGAM (formylglycinamidine ribonucleotide) conversion, which is a key step in purine biosynthesis. In multicomponent systems (found in archaea and some bacteria), this reaction is split between two separate proteins:<br>- A GATase component that hydrolyzes glutamine to release ammonia<br>- A synthetase component that uses that ammonia to convert FGAR to FGAM using ATP<br><br>**Step 3: Evaluate each choice.**<br>- **A**: Describes a GATase that hydrolyzes glutamine to glutamate + ammonia, supplying ammonia to a separate FGAR→FGAM synthetase. This matches a standalone GATase domain in a multicomponent system.<br>- **B**: Describes PRPP amidotransferase (PurF), a different enzyme in purine biosynthesis. - PurQ is the glutamine amidotransferase subunit that hydrolyzes glutamine to produce ammonia, which is then channeled to PurL.
- PurL uses the ammonia, ATP, and FGAR to produce FGAM.
